### Question 27: Chronic Respiratory Acidosis: Severe End-Stage COPD and Emphysema
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Pathophysiology of emphysema leading to chronic respiratory acidosis, specifically the role of elastin destruction in dynamic airway collapse and CO2 retention (肺气肿病理生理学导致慢性呼吸性酸中毒，特别是弹性蛋白破坏在动态气道塌陷和CO2潴留中的作用).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 70-year-old male with a 50-pack-year smoking history presents to the emergency department with worsening dyspnea over the past week. He has known severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and uses supplemental oxygen at home. His current medications include inhaled bronchodilators and corticosteroids. On examination, he is alert but appears fatigued, using accessory muscles of respiration. His respiratory rate is 24 breaths/min, and he has diffuse wheezing and decreased breath sounds bilaterally. Vital signs: BP 130/80 mmHg, HR 110 bpm, RR 24/min, SpO2 88% on 1.5 L/min nasal cannula, Temperature 37.0°C. Arterial blood gas (ABG) analysis on room air (prior to nasal cannula) reveals: pH 7.35, PaCO2 70 mmHg, PaO2 55 mmHg, [HCO3-] 38 mEq/L. Serum chemistry is unremarkable except for mild leukocytosis. Chest X-ray shows hyperinflation, flattened diaphragms, and bullae. Spirometry performed 6 months ago showed FEV1/FVC ratio of 0.35 and FEV1 of 28% predicted. The patient's chronic ABG values typically show a pH around 7.36-7.38, PaCO2 55-65 mmHg, PaO2 55-60 mmHg, and HCO3- 36-40 mEq/L. Which of the following structural pulmonary parenchymal alterations in emphysema is the primary driver of expiratory dynamic airway collapse and subsequent chronic CO2 retention?

(A) Hyperinflation leading to increased intrathoracic pressure and decreased venous return.
(B) Destruction of alveolar capillaries causing ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch and hypoxemia.
(C) Enzymatic destruction of alveolar elastin reducing radial tethering and elastic recoil.
(D) Chronic inflammation leading to airway remodeling and fixed airflow obstruction.
(E) Increased mucus production obstructing small airways and impairing gas exchange.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 70-year-old male, 50-pack-year smoking history, known severe COPD. This points towards emphysema and chronic bronchitis as the underlying pathology.
- **Symptoms:** Worsening dyspnea, fatigue, accessory muscle use, wheezing, decreased breath sounds. Consistent with COPD exacerbation or progression.
- **Vital Signs:** Tachypnea (RR 24), tachycardia (HR 110), hypoxemia (SpO2 88% on 1.5L NC). Indicate respiratory distress and impaired gas exchange.
- **ABG:** pH 7.35 (acidemia), PaCO2 70 mmHg (hypercapnia), PaO2 55 mmHg (hypoxemia), HCO3- 38 mEq/L (elevated). This represents a chronic respiratory acidosis with metabolic compensation. The pH is near normal (7.35) despite significant hypercapnia (PaCO2 70), indicating renal compensation (elevated HCO3-). The chronic nature is supported by the elevated bicarbonate level, which is appropriate for the level of PaCO2 according to Winter's formula (expected HCO3- ≈ 0.3 * 70 + 24 = 45, actual is 38, suggesting some degree of compensation, although perhaps not maximal or potentially a mixed picture if baseline was higher). The patient's typical chronic ABG values (pH 7.36-7.38, PaCO2 55-65, HCO3- 36-40) further confirm chronic respiratory acidosis with compensation.
- **Spirometry:** FEV1/FVC = 0.35, FEV1 = 28% predicted. Confirms severe obstructive lung disease, characteristic of severe emphysema.
- **Chest X-ray:** Hyperinflation, flattened diaphragms, bullae. Classic findings of emphysema.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *primary* structural alteration causing *expiratory dynamic airway collapse* and *chronic CO2 retention*. This focuses on the specific mechanism of airflow limitation in emphysema.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Acid-Base Biophysics (酸碱平衡机制与生物物理推导)
- **Emphysema Pathophysiology:** Emphysema is characterized by the destruction of alveolar walls and enlargement of airspaces distal to the terminal bronchioles. This destruction is primarily caused by an imbalance between proteases (e.g., neutrophil elastase) and antiproteases (e.g., alpha-1 antitrypsin), often exacerbated by smoking.
- **Loss of Elastic Recoil:** The destruction of alveolar walls leads to a loss of elastic recoil in the lung parenchyma. Elastic fibers within the alveolar walls provide the force that normally helps to keep small airways open during expiration.
- **Radial Traction:** Small airways are normally kept open during expiration by the radial traction exerted by the surrounding elastic lung parenchyma. When the parenchyma is destroyed (emphysema), this radial traction is lost.
- **Dynamic Airway Collapse:** During forced expiration, the pressure within the airways increases. Without sufficient elastic recoil and radial traction, these small airways become prone to dynamic collapse (closing during expiration and reopening during inspiration). This collapse significantly increases airflow resistance and leads to air trapping and hyperinflation.
- **CO2 Retention:** The increased airflow resistance and air trapping impair the ability to effectively exhale CO2, leading to hypercapnia and chronic respiratory acidosis.
- **Compensation:** The kidneys compensate for the chronic respiratory acidosis by increasing the reabsorption of bicarbonate (HCO3-) and excreting H+, leading to an elevated serum HCO3- level.
- **Distractor Analysis Relevance:** The question specifically asks about the mechanism of *dynamic airway collapse* and *CO2 retention*. While other factors contribute to COPD pathophysiology (inflammation, mucus, V/Q mismatch), the loss of elastic recoil and radial traction is the key structural change directly responsible for the expiratory airway collapse seen in emphysema.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  Identify the patient's condition: Severe COPD, likely emphysema-predominant, presenting with chronic respiratory acidosis and hypoxemia.
2.  Analyze the ABG: pH 7.35, PaCO2 70 mmHg, HCO3- 38 mEq/L confirms chronic respiratory acidosis with metabolic compensation.
3.  Recall the pathophysiology of emphysema: Destruction of alveolar walls leads to loss of elastic recoil.
4.  Understand the consequence of lost elastic recoil: Loss of radial traction on small airways.
5.  Connect loss of radial traction to airway mechanics: Small airways are prone to dynamic collapse during expiration.
6.  Relate dynamic airway collapse to gas exchange: Impaired expiration leads to air trapping, increased resistance, and CO2 retention (hypercapnia).
7.  Evaluate the options based on this understanding: Option (C) directly describes the loss of elastic recoil and radial tethering, which is the primary mechanism for dynamic airway collapse and CO2 retention in emphysema.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Hyperinflation leading to increased intrathoracic pressure and decreased venous return.** Hyperinflation is a consequence of air trapping in emphysema, but it's not the primary cause of dynamic airway collapse. Increased intrathoracic pressure can decrease venous return, but this is a hemodynamic effect, not the direct cause of the airway problem leading to CO2 retention.
- **(B) Destruction of alveolar capillaries causing ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch and hypoxemia.** Destruction of capillaries does occur in emphysema, leading to V/Q mismatch and hypoxemia (as seen in the patient's PaO2). However, this primarily explains the hypoxemia, not the dynamic airway collapse and hypercapnia, which are driven by the loss of airway support.
- **(C) Enzymatic destruction of alveolar elastin reducing radial tethering and elastic recoil.** This is the correct answer. Elastin provides the elastic recoil necessary to keep airways open. Its destruction by proteases (like elastase) reduces radial traction (the outward pull on airways from the surrounding lung tissue) and elastic recoil, leading to expiratory dynamic airway collapse, air trapping, and CO2 retention.
- **(D) Chronic inflammation leading to airway remodeling and fixed airflow obstruction.** Chronic inflammation is a key feature of COPD, particularly chronic bronchitis, and contributes to airway remodeling (thickening of airway walls, fibrosis). This causes fixed airflow obstruction, but it doesn't fully explain the *dynamic* collapse seen in emphysema, which is more related to the loss of parenchymal support. While inflammation contributes to emphysema, the structural loss of elastin is the specific mechanism for dynamic collapse.
- **(E) Increased mucus production obstructing small airways and impairing gas exchange.** Increased mucus production is characteristic of chronic bronchitis, another component of COPD. While it contributes to airflow obstruction, it is not the primary mechanism for dynamic airway collapse in emphysema, which is driven by parenchymal destruction and loss of elastic support.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                     | Emphysema Pathophysiology                                  | Consequence                                           |
| :-------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- |
| Primary Structural Change   | Destruction of alveolar walls, loss of elastin              | Loss of elastic recoil, loss of radial traction        |
| Airway Mechanics            | Dynamic airway collapse during expiration                  | Air trapping, increased airflow resistance             |
| Gas Exchange Effect         | Impaired CO2 exhalation                                   | Hypercapnia, chronic respiratory acidosis             |
| Key Driver of CO2 Retention | Loss of elastic recoil & radial traction                  | Expiratory dynamic airway collapse                    |
| Other Contributing Factors | Inflammation, mucus hypersecretion (chronic bronchitis)     | Fixed airflow obstruction, V/Q mismatch (capillary loss) |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
In emphysema, the destruction of alveolar walls leads to a loss of lung elastic recoil and radial traction on small airways. This structural change is the primary cause of expiratory dynamic airway collapse, resulting in air trapping, increased airflow resistance, and ultimately, chronic CO2 retention and respiratory acidosis. Understanding this specific mechanism is crucial for differentiating the pathophysiology of emphysema from other obstructive lung diseases.

---

### Question 28: Chronic Respiratory Acidosis: Amyotrophic Lateral Sclerosis (ALS) Diaphragmatic Paresis
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of chronic respiratory acidosis due to neuromuscular weakness (specifically ALS affecting the diaphragm), its characteristic compensation, and the specific diagnostic criteria for diaphragmatic paresis using positional spirometry. (慢性呼吸性酸中毒，神经肌肉疾病（肌萎缩侧索硬化症），膈肌麻痹，体位呼吸功能测试)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 58-year-old man with a known history of amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) presents to the clinic complaining of worsening orthopnea, morning headaches, and excessive daytime sleepiness. He reports feeling refreshed after naps but struggles to stay awake during the day. A nocturnal polysomnography study reveals severe nocturnal hypoventilation with significant hypercapnia. His current daytime arterial blood gas (ABG) analysis, obtained while sitting upright, shows: pH 7.36, PaCO2 55 mmHg, PaO2 85 mmHg, [HCO3-] 30 mEq/L, SpO2 94% on room air. A respiratory function test shows a forced vital capacity (FVC) of 2.5 L when sitting upright. When repositioned supine, his FVC drops to 1.6 L.

What physical finding during positional spirometry confirms severe diaphragmatic weakness in a patient with amyotrophic lateral sclerosis?

(A) A decrease in FVC of less than 10% when moving from sitting to supine.
(B) A decrease in FVC of 15% when moving from sitting to supine.
(C) A decrease in FVC of greater than 25% when moving from sitting to supine.
(D) An increase in FVC when moving from sitting to supine.
(E) A decrease in FEV1/FVC ratio of greater than 20% when moving from sitting to supine.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 58-year-old male with ALS. ALS is a progressive neurodegenerative disease affecting motor neurons, leading to muscle weakness and atrophy.
- **Symptoms:** Orthopnea (difficulty breathing when lying flat), morning headaches (due to nocturnal hypercapnia causing cerebral vasodilation), daytime hypersomnolence (due to sleep fragmentation from hypoventilation and hypercapnia). These symptoms strongly suggest respiratory muscle weakness, particularly affecting ventilation during sleep.
- **Nocturnal Hypoventilation:** Confirmed by polysomnography, indicating impaired ability to maintain adequate alveolar ventilation, especially during REM sleep when respiratory drive decreases and accessory muscles are paralyzed.
- **Daytime ABG:** pH 7.36 (slightly acidic, within normal range but low end), PaCO2 55 mmHg (elevated, indicating respiratory acidosis), [HCO3-] 30 mEq/L (elevated, indicating metabolic compensation for chronic respiratory acidosis). This confirms chronic respiratory acidosis with renal compensation. The compensation is appropriate: using Winter's formula, expected PaCO2 = 1.5 * [HCO3-] + 8 ± 2 = 1.5 * 30 + 8 ± 2 = 45 + 8 ± 2 = 53 ± 2 mmHg. The measured PaCO2 of 55 mmHg falls within this expected range, confirming chronic compensation.
- **Positional Spirometry:** FVC drops from 2.5 L (sitting) to 1.6 L (supine). This is a significant decrease. The question asks for the specific finding that confirms severe diaphragmatic weakness.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Acid-Base Biophysics (酸碱平衡机制与生物物理推导)
- **ALS and Respiratory Failure:** ALS affects motor neurons controlling respiratory muscles, including the diaphragm (innervated by the phrenic nerve) and intercostal muscles. Diaphragmatic weakness is a common cause of respiratory failure in ALS.
- **Nocturnal Hypoventilation Mechanism:** During wakefulness, patients can use accessory respiratory muscles (e.g., sternocleidomastoid, scalenes) to compensate for diaphragmatic weakness. However, during REM sleep, these accessory muscles are paralyzed (atonic), leaving the diaphragm as the primary muscle of respiration. In patients with significant diaphragmatic weakness, this leads to inadequate ventilation and hypercapnia.
- **Chronic Respiratory Acidosis:** Persistent hypoventilation leads to retention of CO2 (PaCO2 ↑), causing respiratory acidosis (pH ↓).
- **Renal Compensation:** The kidneys compensate for chronic respiratory acidosis by increasing the reabsorption of bicarbonate (HCO3-) in the proximal tubules and increasing the generation of new bicarbonate via increased ammoniagenesis. This raises the serum [HCO3-] level, partially offsetting the acidosis and bringing the pH closer to normal. The elevated [HCO3-] (30 mEq/L) in the vignette confirms this compensation.
- **Diaphragmatic Weakness and Positional Spirometry:** The diaphragm is the primary muscle of inspiration. When a person is supine, the abdominal contents (viscera) push upwards against the diaphragm, reducing its excursion and effective volume. In individuals with healthy diaphragms, this positional change causes only a minimal decrease in FVC. However, in patients with diaphragmatic weakness (paresis), the diaphragm is less able to overcome the pressure from the abdominal contents when supine, leading to a significant reduction in FVC. A drop in FVC of >25% from sitting to supine is considered diagnostic of significant diaphragmatic weakness.
- **Winter's Formula:** Used to assess the appropriateness of metabolic compensation in chronic respiratory acidosis. Expected PaCO2 = 1.5 * [HCO3-] + 8 ± 2.
- **Other Acid-Base Considerations:** The vignette focuses on respiratory acidosis. There is no indication of metabolic acidosis (normal anion gap, no mention of lactate, ketones, etc.) or metabolic alkalosis.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the patient's underlying condition: ALS, a disease causing progressive motor neuron degeneration and muscle weakness.
2. Recognize the symptoms: Orthopnea, morning headaches, daytime hypersomnolence, suggesting respiratory muscle weakness and nocturnal hypoventilation.
3. Interpret the ABG: pH 7.36, PaCO2 55 mmHg, [HCO3-] 30 mEq/L. This indicates chronic respiratory acidosis with appropriate metabolic compensation.
4. Understand the mechanism of respiratory failure in ALS: Weakness of respiratory muscles, particularly the diaphragm, leading to hypoventilation, especially during sleep.
5. Relate positional spirometry to diaphragmatic function: Supine position increases the load on the diaphragm. A significant drop in FVC when supine indicates diaphragmatic weakness.
6. Recall the diagnostic threshold: A drop in FVC > 25% from sitting to supine is diagnostic of significant diaphragmatic weakness.
7. Apply this knowledge to the vignette: The patient's FVC dropped from 2.5 L to 1.6 L. Calculate the percentage drop: ((2.5 - 1.6) / 2.5) * 100% = (0.9 / 2.5) * 100% = 36%.
8. Compare the calculated drop to the diagnostic threshold: 36% > 25%.
9. Conclude that the finding confirms severe diaphragmatic weakness.
10. Select the option that matches this diagnostic criterion.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) A decrease in FVC of less than 10% when moving from sitting to supine.** This represents a normal or minimal decrease in FVC, indicating adequate diaphragmatic function. Incorrect.
- **(B) A decrease in FVC of 15% when moving from sitting to supine.** This decrease is less than the threshold (25%) typically used to define significant diaphragmatic weakness. While it might suggest some weakness, it doesn't confirm *severe* weakness as the question asks. Incorrect.
- **(C) A decrease in FVC of greater than 25% when moving from sitting to supine.** This is the established diagnostic criterion for significant diaphragmatic weakness. The patient's FVC dropped by 36%, which meets this criterion. Correct.
- **(D) An increase in FVC when moving from sitting to supine.** An increase in FVC is physiologically implausible and would not indicate diaphragmatic weakness. Incorrect.
- **(E) A decrease in FEV1/FVC ratio of greater than 20% when moving from sitting to supine.** The FEV1/FVC ratio is used to assess obstructive lung disease. While respiratory muscle weakness can affect airflow, a change in the FEV1/FVC ratio is not the primary diagnostic criterion for diaphragmatic weakness assessed by positional spirometry. The key finding is the absolute drop in FVC. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | Normal Diaphragmatic Function | Significant Diaphragmatic Weakness (e.g., ALS) | Diagnostic Test                                  |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------- |
| FVC (Sitting vs. Supine) | Minimal decrease (<10%)       | Significant decrease (>25%)                    | Positional Spirometry                            |
| Mechanism               | Diaphragm effectively expands chest cavity | Diaphragm unable to overcome abdominal pressure when supine | Measures lung volumes in different positions     |
| Clinical Context        | Healthy individual             | Neuromuscular disease (ALS), myasthenia gravis | Used to assess respiratory muscle strength       |
| ABG Findings            | Normal                         | Chronic Respiratory Acidosis (↑PaCO2, ↑HCO3-)   | Reflects chronic hypoventilation                  |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
This question highlights the importance of understanding how neuromuscular diseases like ALS affect respiratory function, specifically diaphragmatic strength. Positional spirometry is a key diagnostic tool to assess diaphragmatic weakness, with a drop in FVC greater than 25% when moving from sitting to supine being indicative of significant paresis. This finding explains the patient's nocturnal hypoventilation and resulting chronic respiratory acidosis with metabolic compensation.

### Question 29: Chronic Respiratory Acidosis: Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome (Pickwickian Syndrome)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating chronic compensated respiratory acidosis due to Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome (OHS) from simple Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) based on daytime awake arterial blood gas (ABG) findings, specifically the presence of chronic hypercapnia. (区分肥胖通气不足综合征（OHS）与单纯阻塞性睡眠呼吸暂停（OSA）的关键在于日间清醒时的ABG，特别是是否存在慢性高碳酸血症。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old man with a history of hypertension and type 2 diabetes mellitus presents to the clinic complaining of excessive daytime sleepiness, loud snoring, and bilateral lower extremity edema. His body mass index (BMI) is 48 kg/m2. Physical examination reveals signs consistent with right heart failure, including jugular venous distension and peripheral pitting edema. An arterial blood gas (ABG) sample is drawn while the patient is awake and breathing room air. The results are: pH 7.34, PaCO2 56 mmHg, PaO2 58 mmHg, [HCO3-] 30 mEq/L, SpO2 88% on room air. Serum electrolytes and renal function are within normal limits. Echocardiography reveals pulmonary hypertension and right ventricular hypertrophy. The patient denies any history of neuromuscular disease, significant lung disease (e.g., COPD, interstitial lung disease), or use of sedating medications.

Which of the following laboratory findings during daytime wakefulness is mandatory to diagnose obesity hypoventilation syndrome (OHS) in this patient, distinguishing it from simple obstructive sleep apnea (OSA)?

(A) Elevated serum bicarbonate concentration ([HCO3-] > 28 mEq/L)
(B) Decreased arterial pH (< 7.35)
(C) Elevated PaCO2 during daytime wakefulness (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg)
(D) Hypoxemia (PaO2 < 60 mmHg)
(E) Elevated serum anion gap (> 12 mEq/L)

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, obese (BMI 48 kg/m2), history of HTN, DM2. This profile is highly suggestive of metabolic syndrome and increased risk for OHS.
- **Symptoms:** Excessive daytime sleepiness, loud snoring, peripheral edema. These are classic symptoms of both OSA and OHS. Edema suggests potential right heart failure secondary to chronic hypoxemia and pulmonary hypertension.
- **Physical Exam:** Signs of right heart failure (JVD, pitting edema). Consistent with chronic hypoxemia leading to pulmonary vasoconstriction, pulmonary hypertension, and cor pulmonale.
- **ABG (Daytime Awake):** pH 7.34 (acidemia), PaCO2 56 mmHg (hypercapnia), PaO2 58 mmHg (hypoxemia), [HCO3-] 30 mEq/L (elevated). This pattern indicates chronic compensated respiratory acidosis. The elevated bicarbonate suggests renal compensation for chronic hypercapnia.
- **Echocardiography:** Pulmonary hypertension and right ventricular hypertrophy. Supports the diagnosis of cor pulmonale secondary to chronic respiratory failure.
- **Exclusion Criteria:** No history of neuromuscular disease, significant lung disease, or sedating medications. This helps rule out other causes of hypoventilation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mandatory finding to distinguish OHS from simple OSA during daytime wakefulness.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Acid-Base Biophysics (酸碱平衡机制与生物物理推导)
- **Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA):** Characterized by recurrent episodes of upper airway obstruction during sleep, leading to intermittent hypoxemia, hypercapnia, and arousals. During wakefulness, ventilation is typically normal, and ABGs are usually within normal limits, although some patients may have mild daytime hypercapnia.
- **Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome (OHS):** Defined by the triad of obesity (BMI ≥ 30 kg/m2), daytime awake hypercapnia (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg), and exclusion of other causes of hypoventilation. The pathophysiology involves increased work of breathing due to excess chest wall and abdominal adipose tissue, blunted central chemoreceptor response to CO2, and potentially leptin resistance affecting respiratory drive.
- **Chronic Respiratory Acidosis:** Occurs when alveolar ventilation is inadequate to eliminate CO2, leading to elevated PaCO2. The body compensates by increasing renal bicarbonate reabsorption, raising serum [HCO3-].
- **Compensation:** In chronic respiratory acidosis, the kidneys retain bicarbonate to buffer the excess acid. The expected compensation can be estimated using Winter's formula: Expected [HCO3-] = 24 + (0.4 * (PaCO2 - 40)). In this case, Expected [HCO3-] = 24 + (0.4 * (56 - 40)) = 24 + (0.4 * 16) = 24 + 6.4 = 30.4 mEq/L. The patient's measured [HCO3-] of 30 mEq/L is consistent with appropriate renal compensation.
- **Distinguishing OHS from OSA:** The key differentiating factor is the presence of *persistent* daytime awake hypercapnia (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg) in OHS, whereas patients with simple OSA typically have normal daytime ABGs or only mild, transient hypercapnia. The patient's PaCO2 of 56 mmHg during wakefulness meets the diagnostic criterion for OHS.
- **Other Findings:**
    - Elevated [HCO3-] (30 mEq/L) is a consequence of chronic respiratory acidosis compensation, present in both OHS and severe OSA, but not diagnostic on its own.
    - Decreased pH (< 7.35) indicates acidemia, present here (7.34), but it's the *cause* (chronic hypercapnia) that distinguishes OHS.
    - Hypoxemia (PaO2 < 60 mmHg) is common in both conditions due to ventilation-perfusion mismatch and alveolar hypoventilation, but not the defining feature for OHS vs. OSA.
    - Elevated anion gap is not typically associated with OHS or OSA unless another condition (like lactic acidosis or ketoacidosis) is present.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the patient's key features: Obese (BMI 48), symptoms of sleep-disordered breathing (sleepiness, snoring), signs of right heart failure, and daytime awake ABG showing chronic compensated respiratory acidosis (low pH, high PaCO2, high HCO3-).
2. Recognize that both OSA and OHS can cause sleep-disordered breathing and potentially lead to chronic respiratory acidosis and cor pulmonale.
3. Recall the diagnostic criteria for OHS, which specifically include daytime awake hypercapnia (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg).
4. Evaluate the patient's daytime awake ABG: PaCO2 is 56 mmHg, which is significantly elevated and meets the criterion for OHS.
5. Consider the options provided:
    - (A) Elevated HCO3- is expected compensation, not specific to OHS vs. OSA.
    - (B) Decreased pH is present but reflects the underlying hypercapnia, not the distinguishing feature.
    - (C) Elevated PaCO2 during daytime wakefulness is the defining criterion for OHS.
    - (D) Hypoxemia is common but not specific.
    - (E) Elevated anion gap is unrelated to the primary respiratory issue.
6. Conclude that the mandatory finding to distinguish OHS from simple OSA is the presence of daytime awake hypercapnia.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Elevated serum bicarbonate concentration ([HCO3-] > 28 mEq/L):** While the patient's bicarbonate is elevated (30 mEq/L), reflecting renal compensation for chronic hypercapnia, this finding is not specific to OHS. Patients with severe OSA can also develop chronic respiratory acidosis and compensatory hyperbicarbonatemia. Therefore, elevated bicarbonate alone does not distinguish OHS from OSA.
- **(B) Decreased arterial pH (< 7.35):** The patient's pH is indeed low (7.34), indicating acidemia. However, this is a consequence of the hypercapnia, not the distinguishing feature between OHS and OSA. Both conditions can lead to acidemia if ventilation is severely impaired. The *cause* of the acidemia (chronic hypercapnia present during wakefulness) is the key differentiator.
- **(C) Elevated PaCO2 during daytime wakefulness (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg):** This is the defining characteristic of OHS according to established diagnostic criteria. Simple OSA typically does not cause persistent hypercapnia during wakefulness, although transient hypercapnia may occur during sleep. The patient's PaCO2 of 56 mmHg clearly meets this criterion, confirming OHS. This is the correct answer.
- **(D) Hypoxemia (PaO2 < 60 mmHg):** The patient is hypoxemic (PaO2 58 mmHg), which is common in both OHS and OSA due to alveolar hypoventilation and V/Q mismatch. However, hypoxemia is not the defining feature that distinguishes OHS from OSA. Many patients with OSA have significant nocturnal hypoxemia but normal daytime oxygenation.
- **(E) Elevated serum anion gap (> 12 mEq/L):** There is no information in the vignette to suggest an elevated anion gap. An elevated anion gap would indicate the presence of unmeasured anions (e.g., ketoacids, lactate, toxins) and is not a characteristic feature of OHS or OSA. The patient's anion gap is likely normal (Na+ - (Cl- + HCO3-) = 140 - (105 + 30) = 5 mEq/L, assuming normal Cl-).

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                     | Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA)                                  | Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome (OHS)                               |
|-----------------------------|-----------------------------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------|
| **Definition**              | Recurrent upper airway obstruction during sleep                  | Obesity (BMI ≥ 30) + Daytime Awake Hypercapnia (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg) + Exclusion of other causes |
| **Daytime ABG (Wakeful)**   | Typically normal PaCO2; may have mild/transient hypercapnia      | **Persistent Hypercapnia (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg)**                        |
| **Nocturnal ABG**           | Intermittent hypoxemia, hypercapnia, arousals                  | Severe, sustained hypoxemia and hypercapnia                         |
| **BMI**                     | Can occur at any BMI, but common in obese individuals            | **BMI ≥ 30 kg/m2**                                                  |
| **Symptoms**                | Snoring, daytime sleepiness, witnessed apneas                    | Snoring, daytime sleepiness, edema, signs of right heart failure    |
| **Compensation**            | May develop chronic respiratory acidosis with elevated HCO3- if severe | Develops chronic respiratory acidosis with elevated HCO3-           |
| **Key Differentiator**      | Normal daytime PaCO2                                            | **Elevated daytime PaCO2 (≥ 45 mmHg)**                             |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Obesity Hypoventilation Syndrome (OHS) is diagnosed based on obesity (BMI ≥ 30 kg/m2), daytime awake hypercapnia (PaCO2 ≥ 45 mmHg), and exclusion of other causes. The critical finding distinguishing OHS from simple Obstructive Sleep Apnea (OSA) is the presence of persistent hypercapnia during daytime wakefulness, reflecting chronic alveolar hypoventilation.

---

### Question 30: Chronic Respiratory Acidosis: Kyphoscoliosis Restrictive Chest Wall Disease
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Chronic respiratory acidosis (慢性呼吸性酸中毒) caused by restrictive lung disease (限制性肺疾病) due to severe chest wall deformity (严重胸廓畸形), specifically kyphoscoliosis (脊柱侧弯), leading to chronic alveolar hypoventilation (慢性肺泡通气不足) and renal compensation (肾脏代偿).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 64-year-old woman with a known history of severe idiopathic thoracolumbar kyphoscoliosis (Cobb angle 85 degrees) presents to the clinic complaining of worsening exertional dyspnea and bilateral lower extremity edema over the past 6 months. Physical examination reveals marked thoracic spinal curvature, decreased chest expansion, and a right ventricular heave on precordial palpation. Vital signs are: BP 130/85 mmHg, HR 90 bpm, RR 24 breaths/min, SpO2 88% on room air, Temperature 37°C. Arterial blood gas (ABG) analysis on room air shows: pH 7.35, PaCO2 58 mmHg, PaO2 55 mmHg, [HCO3-] 31 mEq/L. Serum chemistry panel is unremarkable except for a slightly elevated BUN (25 mg/dL) and creatinine (1.4 mg/dL). Chest X-ray confirms severe kyphoscoliosis with significantly reduced thoracic volume. Pulmonary function tests (PFTs) show a total lung capacity (TLC) of 45% of predicted, with a normal FEV1/FVC ratio. Given the patient's clinical presentation and ABG results, what is the primary pathophysiological alteration responsible for her chronic hypercapnic respiratory acidosis?

(A) Increased sensitivity of the central chemoreceptors to CO2, leading to hyperventilation and subsequent respiratory alkalosis.
(B) Decreased pulmonary vascular resistance due to lung hyperinflation, causing increased pulmonary blood flow and washout of CO2.
(C) Marked reduction in thoracic chest wall compliance causing chronic shallow alveolar hypoventilation.
(D) Increased work of breathing leading to respiratory muscle fatigue and acute respiratory alkalosis followed by central respiratory depression.
(E) Impaired gas exchange due to ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch secondary to pulmonary hypertension caused by chronic hypoxemia.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile**: 64-year-old woman with severe kyphoscoliosis (Cobb angle 85 degrees). This immediately points towards a restrictive chest wall disorder.
- **Symptoms**: Exertional dyspnea and leg swelling. Dyspnea is expected with restrictive lung disease. Leg swelling suggests right heart failure (cor pulmonale), a known complication of chronic hypoxemia and pulmonary hypertension.
- **Physical Exam**: Marked spinal curvature, decreased chest expansion (consistent with restrictive chest wall disease), right ventricular heave (suggests pulmonary hypertension/cor pulmonale).
- **ABG**: pH 7.35 (acidemia), PaCO2 58 mmHg (hypercapnia), PaO2 55 mmHg (hypoxemia), [HCO3-] 31 mEq/L (elevated). This pattern indicates chronic respiratory acidosis with metabolic compensation. The pH is near normal (7.35) despite significant hypercapnia (PaCO2 58), indicating chronic adaptation. The elevated bicarbonate (31 mEq/L) confirms renal compensation for chronic respiratory acidosis.
- **PFTs**: TLC 45% of predicted (restrictive pattern), normal FEV1/FVC ratio (rules out obstructive disease). This confirms severe restrictive lung disease.
- **Question**: Asks for the *primary* pathophysiological alteration causing the *chronic hypercapnic respiratory acidosis*.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Acid-Base Biophysics (酸碱平衡机制与生物物理推导)
- **Restrictive Chest Wall Disease (Kyphoscoliosis)**: Severe spinal curvature (Cobb angle > 60-70 degrees) physically restricts chest wall expansion. This leads to a marked decrease in thoracic compliance (ΔVolume/ΔPressure).
- **Reduced Compliance & Ventilation**: The stiff chest wall makes it difficult to achieve large tidal volumes (Vt). Patients compensate by increasing respiratory rate (RR), but the overall minute ventilation (MV = Vt x RR) is often insufficient, especially during sleep or exertion. The shallow breathing also increases the fraction of dead space ventilation (V_D/Vt), further reducing alveolar ventilation (V_A = MV - V_D).
- **Chronic Alveolar Hypoventilation**: Reduced V_A leads to inadequate removal of CO2 from the alveoli, causing chronic hypercapnia (elevated PaCO2).
- **Respiratory Acidosis**: Elevated PaCO2 shifts the equilibrium of the carbonic acid reaction (CO2 + H2O ⇌ H2CO3 ⇌ H+ + HCO3-) to the right, increasing H+ concentration and lowering pH, resulting in respiratory acidosis.
- **Chronic Adaptation**: Over time, the kidneys compensate for the chronic respiratory acidosis by increasing the reabsorption of filtered bicarbonate (HCO3-) and increasing the excretion of H+ (primarily as NH4+ via ammoniagenesis). This raises the serum [HCO3-] level, partially offsetting the acidosis and bringing the pH closer to normal (compensated respiratory acidosis). Winter's formula can be used to estimate the expected compensation: Expected PaCO2 = 1.5 * [HCO3-] + 8 ± 2 = 1.5 * 31 + 8 ± 2 = 46.5 + 8 ± 2 = 54.5 ± 2 mmHg. The patient's PaCO2 of 58 mmHg is slightly higher than the expected compensation, suggesting a partially compensated state or potentially a superimposed metabolic component (though the clinical picture strongly points to primary respiratory acidosis).
- **Hypoxemia**: Chronic hypoventilation also leads to chronic hypoxemia (low PaO2).
- **Pulmonary Hypertension & Cor Pulmonale**: Chronic hypoxemia causes hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction (HPV), leading to increased pulmonary artery pressure (pulmonary hypertension). This increases the workload on the right ventricle, eventually leading to right ventricular hypertrophy and failure (cor pulmonale), explaining the leg edema and right ventricular heave.
- **Primary Cause**: The fundamental problem initiating this cascade is the mechanical restriction imposed by the severe kyphoscoliosis, leading to reduced chest wall compliance and chronic hypoventilation.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the patient's primary condition: Severe kyphoscoliosis.
2. Recognize kyphoscoliosis as a cause of restrictive chest wall disease.
3. Understand that restrictive chest wall disease reduces chest wall compliance.
4. Deduce that reduced compliance leads to difficulty in achieving adequate tidal volumes.
5. Conclude that inadequate tidal volumes result in chronic shallow breathing and alveolar hypoventilation.
6. Relate alveolar hypoventilation to impaired CO2 removal, leading to hypercapnia.
7. Recognize that hypercapnia causes respiratory acidosis.
8. Note the chronic nature of the condition (severe kyphoscoliosis, elevated HCO3-, near-normal pH) and the resulting renal compensation.
9. Identify the primary pathophysiological alteration as the reduced chest wall compliance causing chronic hypoventilation.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Increased sensitivity of the central chemoreceptors to CO2**: This would lead to *hyperventilation* and *respiratory alkalosis*, the opposite of the patient's condition. Incorrect.
- **(B) Decreased pulmonary vascular resistance due to lung hyperinflation**: Lung hyperinflation is characteristic of obstructive lung disease (e.g., emphysema), not restrictive disease. Restrictive disease is associated with decreased lung volumes. Furthermore, decreased PVR would improve gas exchange, not cause hypercapnia. Incorrect.
- **(C) Marked reduction in thoracic chest wall compliance causing chronic shallow alveolar hypoventilation**: This accurately describes the primary mechanism. The stiff chest wall limits expansion, leading to shallow breaths, reduced alveolar ventilation, CO2 retention, and chronic respiratory acidosis. Correct.
- **(D) Increased work of breathing leading to respiratory muscle fatigue and acute respiratory alkalosis followed by central respiratory depression**: While increased work of breathing occurs, the primary issue is the *mechanical limitation* causing chronic hypoventilation, not acute fatigue leading to central depression. Acute respiratory alkalosis is caused by hyperventilation. Incorrect.
- **(E) Impaired gas exchange due to ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch secondary to pulmonary hypertension caused by chronic hypoxemia**: While V/Q mismatch and pulmonary hypertension (due to chronic hypoxemia from hypoventilation) are present and contribute to hypoxemia, the *primary* cause of the *hypercapnia* (and thus the respiratory acidosis) is the chronic alveolar hypoventilation due to the restrictive chest wall defect. V/Q mismatch primarily causes hypoxemia, although severe mismatch can contribute to hypercapnia, it's not the primary driver here. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                     | Kyphoscoliosis-Induced Respiratory Acidosis | Other Causes of Chronic Respiratory Acidosis |
|-----------------------------|---------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**          | Restrictive Chest Wall Disease (↓ Compliance) | COPD, Neuromuscular Disease, OSA, Obesity Hypoventilation |
| **Ventilation Pattern**     | Shallow, Rapid Breathing (↓ Vt, ↑ RR)      | Variable, often decreased MV                 |
| **Key PFT Finding**         | ↓ TLC, Normal FEV1/FVC                     | ↓ FEV1/FVC (COPD), ↓ FVC (Restrictive)      |
| **Primary Gas Abnormality** | Hypercapnia (↑ PaCO2)                      | Hypercapnia (↑ PaCO2)                      |
| **Compensation**            | Renal ↑ HCO3-                               | Renal ↑ HCO3-                               |
| **Associated Findings**     | Cor Pulmonale, ↓ Chest Expansion           | Chronic Cough, Sputum, Barrel Chest (COPD) |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Severe restrictive chest wall deformities, such as advanced kyphoscoliosis, significantly reduce thoracic compliance, leading to chronic shallow alveolar hypoventilation. This hypoventilation results in CO2 retention, causing chronic hypercapnic respiratory acidosis, which is partially compensated by renal bicarbonate retention. Understanding the mechanical basis of ventilation impairment in restrictive lung disease is crucial for diagnosing and managing these patients.

---

### Question 31: Chronic Respiratory Acidosis: Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome (PHOX2B)
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of congenital central hypoventilation syndrome (CCHS), its underlying genetic cause (PHOX2B mutation), pathophysiology involving impaired central chemoreception during sleep, and the resulting chronic respiratory acidosis with renal compensation. (本题考察先天性中枢性呼吸衰竭综合征 (CCHS) 的理解，包括其潜在的基因原因 (PHOX2B 突变)、病理生理机制 (睡眠期间中枢化学感受受体功能障碍) 以及由此产生的慢性呼吸性酸中毒伴肾脏代偿。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 2-day-old male infant, born full-term (39 weeks gestation) via uncomplicated vaginal delivery, is admitted to the neonatal intensive care unit (NICU) for recurrent episodes of apnea, cyanosis, and bradycardia occurring exclusively during sleep. The infant exhibits normal spontaneous breathing and activity while awake, with no signs of respiratory distress or cyanosis. Physical examination is unremarkable, including a normal neurological assessment, cardiac auscultation, and chest X-ray. Vital signs while awake are stable: HR 140 bpm, RR 50 breaths/min, SpO2 98% on room air. However, during sleep, the infant develops severe apnea lasting up to 5 minutes, accompanied by cyanosis (SpO2 < 85%) and bradycardia (HR < 100 bpm). Arterial blood gas (ABG) analysis obtained during a sleep-induced apneic episode reveals: pH 7.29, PaCO2 65 mmHg, PaO2 50 mmHg, [HCO3-] 27 mEq/L, Base Excess -2 mEq/L. Serum electrolytes, BUN, creatinine, and glucose are within normal limits. A comprehensive workup, including echocardiogram, brain MRI, and genetic testing, is initiated. Genetic testing reveals a polyalanine repeat expansion mutation in the PHOX2B gene. Which of the following genetic mutations is most likely responsible for this infant's condition?

(A) SMN1
(B) CFTR
(C) PHOX2B
(D) ABCC9
(E) PAX6

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Newborn with normal breathing while awake but severe apnea, cyanosis, and bradycardia during sleep. This pattern strongly suggests a defect in the autonomic control of breathing, specifically during sleep.
- **ABG Analysis:** pH 7.29 (acidemia), PaCO2 65 mmHg (high, indicating respiratory acidosis), [HCO3-] 27 mEq/L (elevated, indicating metabolic compensation). The primary disorder is respiratory acidosis (high PaCO2 causing low pH). The elevated bicarbonate suggests chronic compensation by the kidneys to retain bicarbonate in response to chronically elevated PaCO2.
- **Genetic Testing:** Confirms a polyalanine repeat expansion mutation in the PHOX2B gene. This is the definitive diagnostic clue linking the clinical presentation to a specific genetic cause.
- **Differential Diagnosis Consideration:** The vignette rules out structural abnormalities (normal physical exam, chest X-ray, cardiac/brain imaging). The pattern of apnea during sleep points away from conditions like sepsis or metabolic disorders causing generalized apnea.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Acid-Base Biophysics (酸碱平衡机制与生物物理推导)
- **Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome (CCHS):** Also known as Ondine's Curse, CCHS is a rare genetic disorder characterized by impaired autonomic control of breathing, particularly during sleep. Patients fail to respond appropriately to hypercapnia and hypoxia, leading to alveolar hypoventilation.
- **PHOX2B Gene:** The PHOX2B gene encodes a transcription factor crucial for the development of the autonomic nervous system, including the central chemoreceptors located in the retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN) of the brainstem. These chemoreceptors are responsible for sensing changes in PaCO2 and pH and stimulating increased ventilation. Mutations in PHOX2B, typically polyalanine repeat expansions, disrupt the function of these chemoreceptors, leading to the characteristic breathing pattern seen in CCHS.
- **Acid-Base Physiology:** The primary defect is hypoventilation (decreased alveolar ventilation), leading to CO2 retention (increased PaCO2). This causes respiratory acidosis (decreased pH). The body attempts to compensate chronically by increasing renal bicarbonate reabsorption, leading to elevated serum bicarbonate levels ([HCO3-] increases). The degree of compensation can be estimated using Winter's formula (Expected PaCO2 = 1.5 * [HCO3-] + 8 ± 2). In this case, Expected PaCO2 = 1.5 * 27 + 8 ± 2 = 40.5 + 8 ± 2 = 48.5 ± 2 (range 46.5-50.5 mmHg). The measured PaCO2 of 65 mmHg is significantly higher than the expected compensated value, indicating an ongoing, uncompensated or partially compensated respiratory acidosis.
- **Other Options:**
    - **SMN1:** Mutations cause Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA), a neuromuscular disorder affecting motor neurons, leading to muscle weakness and atrophy. Respiratory failure can occur due to diaphragmatic weakness, but the pattern is typically generalized weakness, not specifically sleep-related central apnea.
    - **CFTR:** Mutations cause Cystic Fibrosis (CF), a disorder affecting chloride transport, leading to thick mucus secretions in the lungs and other organs. Respiratory problems in CF are due to airway obstruction, infection, and inflammation, not central hypoventilation.
    - **ABCC9:** Mutations are associated with pseudohypoaldosteronism type II (PHAII), a renal tubular disorder affecting salt reabsorption, leading to salt wasting and dehydration. It does not cause central apnea.
    - **PAX6:** Mutations cause aniridia and other ocular abnormalities. It is not associated with respiratory control defects.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the core clinical problem: Newborn with apnea and cyanosis specifically during sleep, but normal breathing when awake.
2. Recognize this pattern as indicative of a defect in autonomic respiratory control during sleep.
3. Analyze the ABG: Acidemia (pH 7.29) with high PaCO2 (65 mmHg) confirms respiratory acidosis. Elevated HCO3- (27 mEq/L) indicates chronic metabolic compensation.
4. Consider the differential diagnosis for central apnea in newborns, focusing on genetic causes given the unremarkable physical exam and imaging.
5. The vignette provides the definitive answer: Genetic testing reveals a PHOX2B mutation.
6. Link the PHOX2B gene to CCHS, a condition characterized by impaired central chemoreception leading to hypoventilation during sleep and chronic respiratory acidosis.
7. Confirm that the clinical presentation (sleep apnea, cyanosis, bradycardia, chronic respiratory acidosis with compensation) is consistent with CCHS caused by a PHOX2B mutation.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) SMN1:** Incorrect. SMN1 mutations cause Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA). SMA involves progressive muscle weakness due to motor neuron degeneration. While respiratory failure can occur in severe SMA (Type 1), it's typically due to diaphragmatic weakness and generalized respiratory muscle fatigue, not specifically central apnea triggered by sleep. The ABG pattern might show respiratory acidosis, but the underlying mechanism and clinical presentation differ significantly.
- **(B) CFTR:** Incorrect. CFTR mutations cause Cystic Fibrosis (CF). CF primarily affects the lungs through mucus plugging, chronic infection, and inflammation, leading to obstructive lung disease. While respiratory failure can occur, it's not characterized by central apnea during sleep. The ABG might show respiratory acidosis or alkalosis depending on the stage and complications, but the primary pathology is different.
- **(C) PHOX2B:** Correct. PHOX2B mutations are the known genetic cause of Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome (CCHS). CCHS perfectly matches the clinical description: normal breathing while awake, catastrophic central apnea and hypoventilation during sleep, leading to chronic respiratory acidosis with renal bicarbonate retention. The genetic test result confirms this diagnosis.
- **(D) ABCC9:** Incorrect. ABCC9 mutations cause pseudohypoaldosteronism type II (PHAII), a renal tubular disorder affecting electrolyte balance. This condition does not cause central apnea or primary respiratory acidosis.
- **(E) PAX6:** Incorrect. PAX6 mutations are associated with aniridia and other developmental abnormalities, primarily affecting the eye. This gene is unrelated to respiratory control or CCHS.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature             | Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome (CCHS) | Spinal Muscular Atrophy (SMA) | Cystic Fibrosis (CF) | Pseudohypoaldosteronism Type II (PHAII) | Aniridia (PAX6) |
|----------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------|-----------------------|------------------------------------------|-----------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Impaired central chemoreception (PHOX2B mutation) | Motor neuron degeneration (SMN1) | Defective Cl- transport (CFTR) | Renal tubular dysfunction (ABCC9) | Eye development (PAX6) |
| **Respiratory Pattern**| Central apnea during sleep, normal awake breathing | Generalized respiratory muscle weakness | Obstructive/inflammatory lung disease | Normal (unless secondary complications) | Normal |
| **ABG Pattern**      | Chronic respiratory acidosis (high PaCO2, low pH, high HCO3-) | Respiratory acidosis (variable) | Variable (acidosis/alkalosis) | Normal | Normal |
| **Key Gene**         | PHOX2B                                            | SMN1                              | CFTR                  | ABCC9                                    | PAX6            |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Congenital Central Hypoventilation Syndrome (CCHS) is a genetic disorder caused by mutations in the PHOX2B gene, leading to impaired central chemoreception and autonomic control of breathing, particularly during sleep. This results in chronic respiratory acidosis with renal bicarbonate retention, characterized by elevated PaCO2 and HCO3- levels on ABG analysis. The clinical hallmark is normal breathing while awake but life-threatening apnea during sleep. (先天性中枢性呼吸衰竭综合征 (CCHS) 是一种由 PHOX2B 基因突变引起的遗传性疾病，导致中枢化学感受受体功能障碍和自主呼吸控制受损，尤其是在睡眠期间。这会导致慢性呼吸性酸中毒伴肾脏碳酸氢盐潴留，动脉血气分析显示 PaCO2 和 HCO3- 水平升高。临床特征是在清醒时呼吸正常，但在睡眠时出现危及生命的呼吸暂停。)

---

### Question 32: Oxygen-Induced Hypercapnia in COPD
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The primary physiological mechanism responsible for acute worsening of hypercapnia (PaCO2 elevation) in patients with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) when administered high concentrations of supplemental oxygen is the reversal of hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction (HPV), leading to increased ventilation-perfusion (V/Q) mismatch and dead space ventilation. (COPD患者给予高浓度氧气导致急性高碳酸血症的主要生理机制是低氧肺血管收缩的逆转，导致V/Q mismatch加重和无效通气增加。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 68-year-old male with a known history of severe COPD (baseline FEV1 28% predicted) and chronic hypercapnia (baseline PaCO2 55 mmHg, baseline PaO2 52 mmHg on room air) is admitted to the emergency department for an acute exacerbation of bronchitis, presenting with increased dyspnea and sputum production. His vital signs are: BP 110/70 mmHg, HR 115 bpm, RR 28 breaths/min, SpO2 85% on room air. Physical examination reveals diffuse wheezing and prolonged expiratory phase. An arterial blood gas (ABG) drawn while the patient is on room air shows: pH 7.32, PaCO2 60 mmHg, PaO2 50 mmHg, [HCO3-] 32 mEq/L. The attending physician, concerned about the patient's hypoxemia, orders 100% oxygen via a non-rebreather mask at a flow rate of 15 L/min. Approximately one hour later, the patient becomes increasingly somnolent and difficult to arouse. A repeat ABG reveals: pH 7.18, PaCO2 90 mmHg, PaO2 270 mmHg, [HCO3-] 33 mEq/L. Serum electrolytes, renal function, and lactate are unremarkable. What is the PRIMARY physiological mechanism responsible for this acute worsening of hypercapnia?

(A) Blunting of the peripheral chemoreceptor drive by the sudden increase in PaO2.
(B) The Haldane effect, causing increased CO2 release from hemoglobin into the plasma.
(C) Reversal of hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, leading to increased dead space ventilation and worsening V/Q mismatch.
(D) Increased CO2 production due to the increased work of breathing caused by the high oxygen concentration.
(E) Acute respiratory alkalosis secondary to the high PaO2, which suppresses central respiratory drive.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 68-year-old male with severe COPD and chronic hypercapnia (baseline PaCO2 55 mmHg). This indicates chronic respiratory failure with respiratory acidosis partially compensated by renal bicarbonate retention (HCO3- 32 mEq/L).
- **Acute Exacerbation:** Presents with increased dyspnea, sputum, hypoxemia (SpO2 85%, PaO2 50 mmHg), and tachypnea (RR 28). ABG confirms acute-on-chronic respiratory acidosis (pH 7.32, PaCO2 60 mmHg, HCO3- 32 mEq/L).
- **Intervention:** Administration of 100% oxygen via non-rebreather mask.
- **Outcome:** Rapid deterioration in mental status (somnolence, lethargy) and worsening hypercapnia (pH 7.18, PaCO2 90 mmHg) despite significant improvement in PaO2 (270 mmHg). The HCO3- remains relatively stable (33 mEq/L), indicating the change is acute.
- **Key Clue:** The dramatic increase in PaCO2 (from 60 to 90 mmHg) despite marked improvement in PaO2 (from 50 to 270 mmHg) after 100% oxygen administration in a COPD patient points towards oxygen-induced hypercapnia. The question asks for the *primary* mechanism.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Acid-Base Biophysics (酸碱平衡机制与生物物理推导)
- **Hypoxic Pulmonary Vasoconstriction (HPV):** In COPD patients with chronic hypoxemia, pulmonary arterioles in poorly ventilated (low V/Q) lung regions constrict (HPV). This mechanism redirects blood flow away from these poorly ventilated areas towards better-ventilated regions, optimizing gas exchange and minimizing V/Q mismatch.
- **Effect of High FiO2:** Administering 100% oxygen eliminates alveolar hypoxia. This causes the constricted pulmonary arterioles in the poorly ventilated areas to dilate (reversal of HPV).
- **Consequence of HPV Reversal:** Blood flow is now diverted *into* the poorly ventilated lung regions, worsening the V/Q mismatch. More blood perfuses alveoli that are not effectively ventilated, leading to increased dead space ventilation (V_D/V_T ratio increases). This results in a significant increase in PaCO2. This is considered the major contributor (~50%) to oxygen-induced hypercapnia in COPD.
- **Haldane Effect:** Oxygenation of hemoglobin increases its affinity for CO2 and H+, shifting the CO2 dissociation curve to the right. This causes deoxyhemoglobin to release CO2 and H+ into the plasma, acutely increasing dissolved PaCO2. While it contributes (~30%), it's generally considered secondary to HPV reversal.
- **Chemoreceptor Blunting:** Chronic hypoxemia stimulates peripheral chemoreceptors (carotid bodies). Sudden correction of hypoxemia with high FiO2 can transiently reduce this drive, potentially decreasing minute ventilation slightly (~20%). This is a minor contributor compared to HPV reversal.
- **Increased CO2 Production:** While increased work of breathing can increase CO2 production, it's not the primary mechanism for the *acute* and *catastrophic* rise in PaCO2 seen here.
- **Respiratory Alkalosis:** The patient has respiratory *acidosis* (high PaCO2, low pH), not alkalosis. High PaO2 does not cause respiratory alkalosis; it's the *decrease* in PaCO2 that causes respiratory alkalosis.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the patient population: Severe COPD with chronic hypercapnia.
2. Identify the intervention: Administration of 100% oxygen.
3. Identify the outcome: Acute worsening of hypercapnia (PaCO2 increase) despite significant improvement in oxygenation (PaO2 increase).
4. Recall the mechanisms of oxygen-induced hypercapnia in COPD.
5. Evaluate the options:
    - (A) Chemoreceptor blunting is a contributing factor but not the primary one.
    - (B) Haldane effect contributes but is secondary to HPV reversal.
    - (C) Reversal of HPV directly worsens V/Q mismatch and dead space ventilation, leading to increased PaCO2. This is the most significant mechanism.
    - (D) Increased CO2 production is not the primary cause of this acute phenomenon.
    - (E) The patient develops respiratory acidosis, not alkalosis.
6. Conclude that the reversal of HPV is the primary mechanism responsible for the observed clinical picture.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Blunting of the peripheral chemoreceptor drive by the sudden increase in PaO2:** While true that high PaO2 can reduce the hypoxic drive, this effect is relatively minor (~20% contribution) compared to the impact of HPV reversal. The primary driver of the massive PaCO2 increase is the V/Q mismatch worsening. This option describes a real phenomenon but misidentifies it as the *primary* cause.
- **(B) The Haldane effect, causing increased CO2 release from hemoglobin into the plasma:** The Haldane effect does contribute to increased PaCO2 (~30% contribution) when hemoglobin becomes oxygenated. However, it is generally considered the second most important mechanism after HPV reversal. The magnitude of PaCO2 increase seen in this case is primarily attributed to the V/Q mismatch changes.
- **(C) Reversal of hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction, leading to increased dead space ventilation and worsening V/Q mismatch:** This is the correct answer. In COPD patients with chronic hypoxemia, HPV shunts blood away from poorly ventilated areas. Removing the hypoxic stimulus causes these vessels to dilate, redirecting blood flow to these poorly ventilated regions, thereby worsening V/Q mismatch, increasing physiological dead space, and causing a significant rise in PaCO2. This is the most substantial mechanism (~50% contribution).
- **(D) Increased CO2 production due to the increased work of breathing caused by the high oxygen concentration:** While supplemental oxygen might slightly alter the work of breathing, it does not typically cause a massive increase in CO2 production sufficient to explain the PaCO2 rise from 60 to 90 mmHg. The primary issue is impaired CO2 elimination due to worsened V/Q matching, not increased production.
- **(E) Acute respiratory alkalosis secondary to the high PaO2, which suppresses central respiratory drive:** The patient develops acute respiratory *acidosis* (low pH, high PaCO2), not alkalosis. High PaO2 does not cause alkalosis; low PaCO2 does. Furthermore, while severe alkalosis can suppress respiratory drive, this is not the mechanism at play here.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Mechanism                     | Description                                                                                                | Relative Contribution | Key Concept                               |
| :---------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------- | :---------------------------------------- |
| HPV Reversal                 | Dilation of pulmonary arterioles in poorly ventilated lung regions due to removal of hypoxia.                 | ~50%                  | V/Q Mismatch, Dead Space Ventilation     |
| Haldane Effect                | Oxygenation of Hb increases its affinity for CO2, releasing CO2 into plasma.                               | ~30%                  | CO2 Dissociation Curve, Hemoglobin Binding |
| Chemoreceptor Blunting        | Reduction in peripheral chemoreceptor drive due to correction of chronic hypoxemia.                         | ~20%                  | Hypoxic Drive, Minute Ventilation         |
| Increased CO2 Production      | Not a primary mechanism for acute PaCO2 rise in this context.                                               | Minimal               | Metabolic Rate, Work of Breathing         |
| Respiratory Alkalosis         | Incorrect; patient develops respiratory acidosis.                                                            | N/A                  | Acid-Base Physiology                     |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
In patients with chronic COPD and chronic hypoxemia, the administration of high concentrations of supplemental oxygen can paradoxically worsen hypercapnia primarily due to the reversal of hypoxic pulmonary vasoconstriction (HPV). This leads to increased blood flow to poorly ventilated lung regions, worsening V/Q mismatch and increasing physiological dead space, thereby impairing CO2 elimination. Clinicians must carefully titrate oxygen therapy in these patients to maintain adequate oxygenation (target SpO2 88-92%) while minimizing the risk of exacerbating hypercapnia.

---

### Question 33: Post-Hypercapnic Metabolic Alkalosis
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: 理解慢性呼吸性酸中毒患者接受机械通气时，因快速降低PaCO2而暴露出的代谢性碱中毒 (Post-Hypercapnic Metabolic Alkalosis)，以及其背后的生理机制 (肾脏对HCO3-的滞留)。

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 70-year-old woman with a known history of severe chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) and baseline arterial blood gas (ABG) values of pH 7.38, PaCO2 65 mmHg, and [HCO3-] 36 mEq/L is admitted to the intensive care unit (ICU) for community-acquired pneumonia. She is intubated and placed on mechanical ventilation with a tidal volume of 500 mL and a respiratory rate of 16 breaths/min, targeting a "normal" PaCO2 of 40 mmHg. Six hours after intubation, a repeat ABG shows pH 7.56, PaCO2 38 mmHg, PaO2 120 mmHg (on FiO2 40%), and [HCO3-] 34 mEq/L. The patient subsequently develops muscle twitching, hyperreflexia, and new-onset atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response. Which of the following iatrogenic interventions most likely caused this patient's current clinical presentation?

(A) Rapid correction of chronic hypercapnia leading to a transient respiratory alkalosis, which then evolved into metabolic acidosis due to renal compensation.
(B) Overly aggressive mechanical ventilation causing rapid washout of chronic CO2, unmasking a pre-existing severe metabolic alkalosis that was previously compensated for by respiratory acidosis.
(C) Administration of bicarbonate-containing intravenous fluids during resuscitation for pneumonia, leading to a primary metabolic alkalosis superimposed on her chronic respiratory acidosis.
(D) Hypoxemia secondary to pneumonia causing a mixed respiratory and metabolic acidosis, which was then exacerbated by the mechanical ventilation settings.
(E) Renal failure secondary to sepsis from pneumonia, leading to accumulation of metabolic acids and a high anion gap metabolic acidosis, which was worsened by the ventilator settings.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 70-year-old woman with severe chronic COPD. This indicates chronic respiratory acidosis.
- **Baseline ABG:** pH 7.38 (near normal), PaCO2 65 mmHg (high, indicating respiratory acidosis), [HCO3-] 36 mEq/L (high, indicating renal compensation for chronic respiratory acidosis). The compensation is appropriate (Winter's formula: Expected HCO3- = 0.3 * PaCO2 + 24 = 0.3 * 65 + 24 = 19.5 + 24 = 43.5 mEq/L; actual HCO3- is 36 mEq/L, which is lower than expected, suggesting some degree of acute-on-chronic respiratory acidosis or other factors, but the key point is elevated baseline HCO3- due to chronic compensation).
- **Acute Event:** Intubation and mechanical ventilation targeting a "normal" PaCO2 (40 mmHg). This implies rapid reduction of PaCO2.
- **Post-Ventilation ABG:** pH 7.56 (alkalotic), PaCO2 38 mmHg (low, indicating respiratory alkalosis relative to baseline), [HCO3-] 34 mEq/L (still elevated compared to normal, but lower than baseline).
- **Clinical Manifestations:** Neuromuscular irritability (twitching, hyperreflexia) and cardiac arrhythmias (atrial fibrillation). These are classic signs of severe metabolic alkalosis.
- **Key Clue:** The patient has chronic compensated respiratory acidosis (high baseline PaCO2 and HCO3-). Rapid mechanical ventilation lowers PaCO2 quickly. The kidneys take 48-72 hours to excrete the excess bicarbonate accumulated during chronic compensation. Therefore, rapidly lowering PaCO2 exposes the underlying high bicarbonate level, resulting in severe metabolic alkalosis (post-hypercapnic metabolic alkalosis). The pH is alkalotic (7.56), PaCO2 is low (38 mmHg), and HCO3- is still elevated (34 mEq/L) compared to normal, consistent with metabolic alkalosis.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Acid-Base Biophysics (酸碱平衡机制与生物物理推导)
- **Chronic Respiratory Acidosis Compensation:** In chronic hypercapnia (e.g., COPD), the kidneys increase bicarbonate reabsorption and hydrogen ion excretion to compensate, raising plasma [HCO3-] to maintain a near-normal pH. The expected increase in [HCO3-] is approximately 3-4 mEq/L for every 10 mmHg increase in PaCO2 above 40 mmHg.
- **Mechanical Ventilation in Chronic Hypercapnia:** When a patient with chronic compensated respiratory acidosis is mechanically ventilated to a "normal" PaCO2 (around 40 mmHg), the PaCO2 drops rapidly (within hours).
- **Renal Lag:** The kidneys cannot excrete the excess bicarbonate accumulated during chronic compensation quickly enough to match the rapid decrease in PaCO2. This process typically takes 48-72 hours.
- **Post-Hypercapnic Metabolic Alkalosis:** The rapid decrease in PaCO2 exposes the high plasma [HCO3-] level, leading to a sudden and severe metabolic alkalosis. The pH becomes alkalotic, while the PaCO2 is low (respiratory alkalosis component), and the [HCO3-] remains elevated (metabolic alkalosis component).
- **Clinical Consequences:** Severe metabolic alkalosis causes hypokalemia (due to intracellular shift of K+), hypocalcemia (decreased ionized calcium), and increased neuronal excitability, leading to neuromuscular irritability (tetany, seizures) and cardiac arrhythmias.
- **Winter's Formula:** Used to assess compensation in respiratory acidosis. Expected [HCO3-] = 0.3 * (PaCO2 - 40) + 24. In this case, baseline expected [HCO3-] = 0.3 * (65 - 40) + 24 = 0.3 * 25 + 24 = 7.5 + 24 = 31.5 mEq/L. The actual baseline [HCO3-] was 36 mEq/L, indicating some degree of acute-on-chronic or other factors, but the principle of elevated HCO3- due to chronic compensation remains. Post-ventilation, the pH is 7.56, PaCO2 is 38 mmHg. Using Winter's formula to check compensation for respiratory alkalosis: Expected [HCO3-] = 0.3 * (40 - 38) + 24 = 0.3 * 2 + 24 = 0.6 + 24 = 24.6 mEq/L. The actual [HCO3-] is 34 mEq/L, which is significantly higher than expected, confirming a superimposed metabolic alkalosis.
- **Davenport Diagram:** Would show a trajectory moving upwards (increasing pH) and to the left (decreasing PaCO2) from the baseline state, landing in the metabolic alkalosis region.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. Identify the patient's baseline condition: Severe COPD with chronic compensated respiratory acidosis (high PaCO2, high HCO3-, near-normal pH).
2. Identify the intervention: Mechanical ventilation targeting a normal PaCO2 (40 mmHg).
3. Recognize the physiological consequence: Rapid reduction of PaCO2 before the kidneys can excrete the chronically retained bicarbonate.
4. Predict the resulting acid-base disturbance: Unmasking of a severe metabolic alkalosis (post-hypercapnic metabolic alkalosis).
5. Confirm with post-intervention ABG: pH is high (alkalotic), PaCO2 is low (respiratory alkalosis), HCO3- is still elevated (metabolic alkalosis).
6. Correlate with clinical signs: Neuromuscular irritability and cardiac arrhythmias are consistent with severe metabolic alkalosis.
7. Evaluate the options based on this deduction. Option (B) accurately describes this process.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Rapid correction of chronic hypercapnia leading to a transient respiratory alkalosis, which then evolved into metabolic acidosis due to renal compensation.** Incorrect. Rapidly lowering PaCO2 causes respiratory alkalosis, not metabolic acidosis. While the kidneys will eventually compensate for the respiratory alkalosis by excreting bicarbonate, the primary immediate problem is the unmasking of the pre-existing high bicarbonate level from chronic respiratory acidosis compensation, leading to metabolic alkalosis. The pH is alkalotic, not acidotic.
- **(B) Overly aggressive mechanical ventilation causing rapid washout of chronic CO2, unmasking a pre-existing severe metabolic alkalosis that was previously compensated for by respiratory acidosis.** Correct. This accurately describes the pathophysiology of post-hypercapnic metabolic alkalosis. The chronic respiratory acidosis led to renal retention of bicarbonate. Rapidly lowering PaCO2 exposes this high bicarbonate level, causing severe metabolic alkalosis. The respiratory acidosis component was masking the metabolic alkalosis.
- **(C) Administration of bicarbonate-containing intravenous fluids during resuscitation for pneumonia, leading to a primary metabolic alkalosis superimposed on her chronic respiratory acidosis.** Incorrect. While bicarbonate administration can cause metabolic alkalosis, the vignette doesn't mention this. The primary cause here is the rapid change in ventilation and the resulting unmasking of the pre-existing bicarbonate excess. The baseline ABG already showed elevated HCO3-, consistent with compensation for chronic respiratory acidosis, not a primary metabolic alkalosis masked by respiratory acidosis.
- **(D) Hypoxemia secondary to pneumonia causing a mixed respiratory and metabolic acidosis, which was then exacerbated by the mechanical ventilation settings.** Incorrect. The post-ventilation ABG shows PaO2 is 120 mmHg, indicating adequate oxygenation, not hypoxemia. Furthermore, the pH is alkalotic (7.56), not acidotic. The ventilation settings, while aggressive, caused alkalosis, not acidosis.
- **(E) Renal failure secondary to sepsis from pneumonia, leading to accumulation of metabolic acids and a high anion gap metabolic acidosis, which was worsened by the ventilator settings.** Incorrect. There is no information suggesting renal failure. The vignette provides no data on BUN or creatinine to support renal failure. The pH is alkalotic, not acidotic. The anion gap is not provided, but the clinical picture points strongly towards metabolic alkalosis, not metabolic acidosis.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | Post-Hypercapnic Metabolic Alkalosis                                  | Primary Metabolic Alkalosis                                      | Primary Respiratory Acidosis                                   |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ |
| **Cause**               | Rapid reduction of PaCO2 in chronic hypercapnia (renal lag)           | Excess HCO3- (e.g., vomiting, diuretics) or loss of H+        | Increased PaCO2 (e.g., COPD, hypoventilation)                 |
| **Baseline**            | Chronic respiratory acidosis (high PaCO2, high HCO3-)                 | Normal or high HCO3-                                            | High PaCO2, normal or high HCO3- (compensated)                |
| **Intervention**        | Over-aggressive ventilation (low PaCO2)                               | N/A                                                             | N/A                                                           |
| **Post-Intervention ABG** | High pH, Low PaCO2, High HCO3- (relative to normal)                 | High pH, Normal PaCO2, High HCO3-                               | Low pH, High PaCO2, Normal/High HCO3- (compensated)           |
| **Key Mechanism**       | Renal lag in excreting excess HCO3- accumulated during chronic compensation | Primary gain of HCO3- or loss of H+                             | Impaired CO2 elimination                                      |
| **Clinical Signs**      | Neuromuscular irritability, arrhythmias (due to alkalosis)             | Neuromuscular irritability, arrhythmias (due to alkalosis)       | Fatigue, headache (due to hypercapnia)                        |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Patients with chronic compensated respiratory acidosis (high baseline PaCO2 and HCO3-) require careful mechanical ventilation. Overly aggressive ventilation that rapidly lowers PaCO2 before the kidneys can excrete the retained bicarbonate can unmask a severe metabolic alkalosis, leading to significant clinical complications like neuromuscular irritability and arrhythmias. The key is to ventilate these patients to their chronic baseline PaCO2, not a "normal" value of 40 mmHg.
